Simpler Presentations for Many Fragments of Quantum Circuits
Colin Blake

Abstract
Equational reasoning is central to quantum circuit optimisation and verification: one replaces subcircuits by provably equivalent ones using a fixed set of rewrite rules viewed as equations. We study such reasoning through finite equational theories, presenting restricted quantum gate fragments as symmetric monoidal categories (PROPs), where wire permutations are treated as structural and separated cleanly from fragment-specific gate axioms. For six widely used near-Clifford fragments: qubit Clifford, real Clifford, Clifford+T (up to two qubits), Clifford+CS (up to three qubits) and CNOT-dihedral, we transfer the completeness results of prior work into our PROP framework. Beyond completeness, we address minimality (axiom independence). Using uniform separating interpretations into simple semantic targets, we prove minimality for several fragments (including all arities for qubit Clifford, real Clifford, and CNOT-dihedral), and bounded minimality for the remaining cases. Overall, our presentations significantly reduce rule counts compared to prior work and provide a reusable categorical framework for constructing complete and often minimal rewrite systems for quantum circuit fragments.

1
Introduction

Optimisation and verification of quantum circuits are typically performed by

equational reasoning
: a circuit is transformed by replacing a subcircuit with another
and justifying the replacement by a finite set of circuit identities
[
12
,
10
]
. For a fixed gate signature,
this practice is formalised as an
equational theory
, namely a set of well-typed equations
between circuits, closed under sequential and parallel composition
[
11
,
18
,
14
]
. With respect to to an intended interpretation in terms of unitary operators, a rule set is

complete
if it derives every equality between circuits that have the same denotation.

In addition to completeness, we study
axiom independence
and
minimality

[
7
,
2
,
20
]
. An axiom is
independent if it is not derivable from the other axioms of the same theory, and a finite
presentation is minimal if all its axioms are independent. Minimal presentations are of foundational
interest because they isolate the irreducible algebraic content of a fragment; they are also relevant
for automated rewriting and proof search, where redundant rules increase the search space and
complicate termination and confluence analysis
[
13
,
9
]
.

Finite, complete presentations are known for several fragments close to the Clifford
group, including
n
-qubit Clifford operators

[
19
]
, real Clifford
[
17
]
, CNOT-dihedral
[
1
]
,

2
-qubits Clifford+T
[
3
]
,

3
-qubits Clifford+CS
[
4
]
,
and
n
-qutrits Clifford
[
15
]
.
Moreover, for universal circuit languages, finite complete axiomatisations are
known
[
8
,
6
]
and require rules of unbounded arity
[
7
]
. This motivates a
systematic study of fragments in which one can obtain presentations that are simultaneously finite,
complete, and minimal.

To formalise equational reasoning, we work with a standard string-diagrammatic syntax in
which a circuit has a fixed number of wires, and the two operations of sequential and parallel
composition are primitive
[
11
,
18
]
. Many circuit
presentations treat permutations implicitly, allowing wire crossings implicitly without counting them as
gates. We make this convention explicit by working in a setting in which swaps are available as

structural
morphisms and satisfy the usual coherence axioms for symmetric monoidal
categories
[
16
,
11
,
18
]
.
In
\cref
sec:circuits we formalise this distinction using the PRO/PROP terminology: a PRO has only
sequential and parallel composition, while a PROP additionally provides symmetry as part of the ambient structure
[
16
,
14
,
11
]
.

2

\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:0\to 0,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:1\to 1,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CS.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:2\to 2

2

\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:0\to 0,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/X.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/T.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:1\to 1,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:2\to 2

3

\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/wb.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:0\to 0,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:1\to 1,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:2\to 2

Table 1:
Gate signatures studied in this paper.

Contributions.

This paper revisits the six fragments listed in
\cref
tab:fragments. Our starting point is that completeness is already established in the
literature for these fragments, and our aim is not to re-prove fragment-specific normal-form
theorems. Instead, we reduce the number of relations while preserving the same strict unitary
semantics, and we then study the independence of the resulting axioms.

1.

Smaller finite presentations with unchanged semantics.

For each fragment we give in
\cref
sec:circuits a finite presentation with
fewer non-structural axioms than the standard presentations, while preserving completeness for the
strict unitary semantics defined in
\cref
sec:circuits-relations.
Since the source presentations are
already complete, completeness is treated as an invariant that our reductions must preserve,
rather than as a new theorem about the semantics itself. We do so in
\cref
sec:completeness.

2.

Independence and minimality.

In
\cref
sec:minimality, we then analyse the resulting axiom sets and prove axiom independence where possible, establishing minimality of our presentations. Concretely, our presentations for qubit Clifford, real Clifford, and
CNOT-dihedral are minimal (irredundant) in all arities; our Clifford+T presentation is minimal
up to one qubit; our Clifford+CS presentation is minimal up to two qubits; and our qutrit
Clifford presentation is minimal up to two qutrits. The independence
arguments are uniform: for each axiom we exhibit a separating model
[
5
]
, i.e. a strict symmetric
monoidal interpretation into a small target PROP that satisfies all the other axioms but
distinguishes the two sides of the chosen axiom.

Comparison of rule counts: previous work vs. our presentations. Several previous presentations work in a PRO setting where wire permutations are implicit. In our PROP setting these symmetries are structural and not counted as axioms, so raw rule counts
are not strictly comparable.

2
Graphical languages for quantum circuits

This section fixes the categorical syntax and the proof notion used throughout the paper.
Circuits are formalised as morphisms in strict symmetric monoidal categories whose objects are wire counts.
A circuit fragment is specified by a gate signature together with a set of equations, yielding a presented
PROP. Completeness will be formulated as faithfulness of an interpretation functor into a semantic PROP.

2.1
Graphical languages

We work with the one-object-per-wire formalisms PROs and PROPs in the sense of
Joyal–Street
[
11
,
18
]
.
We adopt strictness throughout: associativity
and unit constraints are treated as literal equalities, so parentheses can be omitted.

Definition 1
.

A
PRO
is a strict monoidal category
P
whose objects are the
natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
and whose tensor on objects is addition

n\otimes m\coloneqq n+m
with unit
0
. We write
\textbf{{P}}(n,m)
for morphisms

n\to m
, with sequential composition
\circ
and parallel composition
\otimes
,
subject to the usual strict monoidal axioms
(
\cref
eq:seqidentity,eq:seqassociativity,eq:parassociativity,eq:paridentity,eq:interchange).

We write
f:n\to m
when
f\in\textbf{{P}}(n,m)
;
n
and
m
are the numbers of input
and output wires of
f
. In particular
\mathrm{id}_{n}=\mathrm{id}_{1}^{\otimes n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 2
.

A
PROP
is a PRO
P
equipped with a symmetric monoidal structure,
i.e. symmetry isomorphisms
\sigma_{n,m}:n+m\to m+n

(n,m\in\mathbb{N})
generated by
the basic swap
\sigma\coloneqq\sigma_{1,1}
and satisfying the axioms
(
8
)-(
8
) in
\cref
fig:coherenceprop. We
write
\sigma_{n}\coloneqq\sigma_{n,1}:n+1\to 1+n
for swapping one wire with a block
of
n
wires.

Thus in a PROP, swaps and permutations are part of the ambient structure.
PROPs admit a convenient string-diagram representation: a morphism
f:n\to m

is drawn as a box with
n
incoming wires and
m
outgoing wires. Sequential
composition is drawn by stacking boxes, tensor by placing them side by side, and

\sigma_{n,m}
by crossing a block of
n
wires with a block of
m
wires. With these conventions,
the coherence axioms are exactly the diagrammatic moves that make deformation sound.

f

\mathrm{id}_{0}

\mathrm{id}_{1}

\mathrm{id}_{n}

\sigma

\sigma_{n}

g\circ f

f\otimes g

From now on, equalities obtained solely by these coherence
moves (including permutations) are treated as
structural
and used implicitly in diagrammatic
arguments.

\captionsetup

{subfigure}

f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{0}=f=\mathrm{id}_{0}\otimes f

\sigma\circ\sigma=\mathrm{id}_{2}

\sigma_{|f|}\circ(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{1})=(\mathrm{id}_{1}\otimes f)\circ\sigma_{|f|}

Figure 8:
\cref
eq:seqidentity,eq:seqassociativity,eq:parassociativity,eq:paridentity,eq:interchange are the coherence laws for PROs and

\cref
eq:involution,eq:naturality are the additional coherence laws for PROPs.

2.2
Free PROPs and presentations

Circuit fragments are specified by
generators and relations
. Generators represent primitive
gates (with fixed arity), and relations represent the rewrite rules (axioms) available for equational
reasoning.

Definition 3
.

A
(PROP) signature
is a set
\Sigma
equipped with arity functions

\mathrm{dom},\mathrm{cod}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{N}
.
An element
g\in\Sigma
is written as a generating morphism
g:\mathrm{dom}(g)\to\mathrm{cod}(g)
.

Definition 4
.

Let
\Sigma
be a signature. The
free PROP
on
\Sigma
, written
\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}
, is the
PROP whose morphisms are string diagrams generated from:

•

the non-structural generators
g\in\Sigma
(with their prescribed arities), and

•

the structural morphisms of a PROP (identities and symmetries),

quotiented by PROP coherence (i.e. the axioms of
\cref
fig:coherenceprop).

Equations between diagrams are imposed by quotienting
\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}
by a congruence.

Definition 5
.

Let
\Sigma
be a signature and let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of well-typed equations

L=R
between morphisms of the free PROP
\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}
.
The
presented PROP

\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}
is the quotient of
\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}

by the smallest congruence that contains
\mathcal{R}
and is closed under
\circ
,
\otimes
,
and symmetry.

This quotient is the formal object that we treat as our

graphical language
: diagrams are equal exactly when their equality is derivable
from the coherence axioms together with the chosen relations.

2.3
Monoidal functors and completeness

To connect a presented syntax to its intended meaning, we interpret generators as concrete operations
and extend compositionally. Completeness will assert that the chosen relations capture exactly the
equalities that hold in the intended semantics.

Definition 6
.

A
PROP morphism
is a strict symmetric monoidal functor

F:\textbf{{P}}_{1}\to\textbf{{P}}_{2}
between PROPs that is the identity on objects.
Equivalently,
F
preserves identities, symmetries, sequential composition and
tensor.

Definition 7
.

Let
P
be a PROP.
A
subPROP
of
P
is a wide strict symmetric monoidal subcategory

\textbf{{Q}}\subseteq\textbf{{P}}
.
Concretely, since the objects of a PROP are fixed to be
\mathbb{N}
, this amounts to specifying
subsets

\textbf{{Q}}(n,m)\subseteq\textbf{{P}}(n,m)\;(n,m\in\mathbb{N})

such that
\mathrm{id}_{n}\in\textbf{{Q}}(n,n)
for all
n
;
\sigma_{n,m}\in\textbf{{Q}}(n+m,m+n)
for all
n,m
; and

Q
is closed under
\circ
and
\otimes
.

In this situation, the inclusion functor
\textbf{{Q}}\hookrightarrow\textbf{{P}}
is a faithful PROP morphism.

Definition 8
.

Let
\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}
be a presented PROP and let
C
be a
symmetric monoidal category. An
interpretation functor
is a strict
symmetric monoidal functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}\to\textbf{{C}}

specified on generators
g\in\Sigma
and extended monoidally to all diagrams.

If every equation in
\mathcal{R}
holds in
C
, then

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket
factors uniquely through the quotient, yielding a functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}\to\textbf{{C}}.

Definition 9
.

Let
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}\to\textbf{{C}}
be such an
interpretation. We say that the graphical language
\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}

is
complete
for this semantics if, for all circuits
C_{1},C_{2}
,
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket
implies

\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
. Equivalently, the interpretation
functor is faithful.

We will also use a bounded variant. Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that

\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}
is
complete up to
k
wires
if the
implication holds whenever
C_{1},C_{2}:n\to m
with
n,m\leq k
. Equivalently, the
interpretation functor is faithful on each hom-set

\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}(n,m)
for
n,m\leq k
.

Operationally, completeness says that semantic equality can always be witnessed by an explicit
diagrammatic derivation from the chosen relations; the bounded form restricts this requirement to
circuits of limited arity.

When
C
is (by definition) the symmetric monoidal subcategory of some
ambient category generated by the images of the generators, the interpretation
functor is also full; in that case
\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma}/\mathcal{R}
is actually
isomorphic to the semantic subPROP
C
.

Remark 10
.

Several completeness results in the literature are stated for PROs.
Since we work in PROPs, we record a uniform lifting principle.

Let
\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}
be a complete presented PRO with an interpretation into a
symmetric
monoidal category
C

Assume there exists a PRO circuit

\tau:2\to 2
such that
\left\llbracket\tau\right\rrbracket
is the symmetry
\sigma_{1,1}
of

C
.
Form the free PROP on the same generators, and quotient it by
\mathcal{R}
together with the single equation

\sigma_{1,1}=\tau
.
Define a translation
T
from PROP diagrams to PRO diagrams by recursively replacing each occurrence of the
structural swap
\sigma_{1,1}
by
\tau
(leaving all generators unchanged).
Then
\left\llbracket D\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket T(D)\right\rrbracket
for every PROP diagram
D
, and any semantic equality between PROP diagrams
reduces to a semantic equality between their PRO translations.
If the original PRO presentation is complete for
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket
, then the resulting PROP presentation is complete
for the same semantics.

3
Quantum circuits and their relations

This section fixes the concrete circuit syntax and the strict unitary semantics used throughout the paper.
For qubits, we introduce a single
master signature
containing all gates needed across the qubit fragments;
each fragment will then be obtained by restriction to a sub-signature and by quotienting by a fragment-specific
finite rule set. For qutrits, only the Clifford fragment is considered, so we present it directly.

Definition 11
.

The
qubit master signature

\Sigma^{(2)}
consists of the following
non-structural generators:

\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/minus.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:0\to 0,\qquad\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/Z.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/T.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/X.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:1\to 1,\qquad\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CS.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:2\to 2.

We write
\textbf{{P}}^{(2)}\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma^{(2)}}
for the free PROP on

\Sigma^{(2)}
. Structural morphisms
\mathrm{id}_{n}
and
\sigma_{n,m}
come from the
ambient PROP structure.

Note that every generator
g\in\Sigma^{(2)}
satisfies
\mathrm{dom}(g)=\mathrm{cod}(g)
.
Hence every well-typed diagram in
\textbf{{P}}^{(2)}
has type
n\to n
for some
n
,
and equational derivations never change the wire count. We exploit this arity
preservation in
\cref
sec:minimality.

Let
FdHilb
be the symmetric monoidal category of finite-dimensional
complex Hilbert spaces and linear maps, with tensor product
\otimes
, tensor
unit
\mathbb{C}
, and symmetry given by swapping tensor factors. By Mac Lane’s
coherence theorem
[
16
]
we may regard
FdHilb
as strict monoidal.

Definition 12
.

Let
H,K
be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. A linear map
U:H\to K
is
unitary
if

U^{\dagger}U=\mathrm{id}_{H}
and
UU^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{K},

where
U^{\dagger}
denotes the Hilbert adjoint of
U
.
We write
\mathrm{U}(H,K)
for the set of unitary maps
H\to K
, and
\mathrm{U}(H)\coloneqq\mathrm{U}(H,H)
.

Definition 13
.

Define
Qubit
to be the PROP with objects
\mathbb{N}
and hom-sets

\textbf{{Qubit}}(n,m)\coloneqq\mathrm{U}\big((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n},(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes m}\big)\;(n,m\in\mathbb{N}),

with composition, identities, tensor, and symmetry inherited from
FdHilb
(viewed as strict monoidal).
Define
Qutrit
similarly, replacing
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

We use the standard Dirac notation for computational basis states: for
x\in\{0,1\}
we write
\left|x\right\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
and for
x,y\in\{0,1\}
we abbreviate
\left|x,y\right\rangle\coloneqq\left|x\right\rangle\otimes\left|y\right\rangle
(similarly,

\left|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\right\rangle\coloneqq\left|x_{1}\right\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\left|x_{n}\right\rangle
).

Both
Qubit
and
Qutrit
are endomorphism-only: there are no unitaries

n\to m
for
n\neq m
.

By the universal property of the free PROP, there is a unique strict
symmetric monoidal functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\textbf{{P}}^{(2)}\to\textbf{{Qubit}}

sending:

\begin{array}[]{l l}\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:1\longmapsto e^{i\pi/4}&\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\left|x\right\rangle\longmapsto\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\!\sum_{k=0}^{1}(-1)^{xk}\left|k\right\rangle\\
\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/minus.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:1\longmapsto-1&\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\left|x\right\rangle\longmapsto i^{x}\left|x\right\rangle\\
\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/X.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\left|x\right\rangle\longmapsto\left|1-x\right\rangle&\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/Z.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\left|x\right\rangle\longmapsto(-1)^{x}\left|x\right\rangle\\
\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\left|x,y\right\rangle\longmapsto\left|x,x\oplus y\right\rangle&\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/T.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\left|x\right\rangle\longmapsto e^{i\pi x/4}\left|x\right\rangle\\
\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CS.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket^{(2)}:\left|x,y\right\rangle\longmapsto i^{xy}\left|x,y\right\rangle\end{array}

for
x,y\in\{0,1\}
and addition
\oplus
modulo 2.

3.1
Qubit circuit fragments as presented subPROPs

Each qubit fragment in this paper is specified by a sub-signature of
\Sigma^{(2)}
and by a finite rule set.
Syntactically, this yields a quotient of a free PROP; semantically, it yields the corresponding subPROP of

Qubit
generated by the chosen gates.

Throughout the rest of this subsection, the placeholder
\bullet
ranges over the

five qubit fragments
considered here:

\bullet\in\{\mathit{Cliff},\mathit{RCliff},\mathit{CliffT},\mathit{CliffCS},\mathit{CNOTdihe}\}.

Definition 14
.

For any set
G
of morphisms in
P
, we write
\langle G\rangle\subseteq\textbf{{P}}

for the smallest subPROP containing all structural morphisms and all elements of
G
.

Definition 15
.

Fix a sub-signature
\Sigma_{\bullet}\subseteq\Sigma^{(2)}
.
Let
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma_{\bullet}}
be the free PROP on
\Sigma_{\bullet}
,
and identify it with its canonical image
in
\textbf{{P}}^{(2)}
.
A
rule set
for the fragment
\bullet
is a finite set
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
of well-typed equations between
morphisms of
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}
. We write

\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}/\textup{C}_{\bullet}

for the presented PROP obtained by quotienting by the congruence generated by
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
.

Thus
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}
is the syntax of the fragment: morphisms are circuits built
from
\Sigma_{\bullet}
, quotiented by the chosen equations
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
(with PROP
structure and symmetries treated as structural).

For each fragment
\bullet
we first fix its intended unitary semantics as the subPROP

\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\bullet}\coloneqq\big\langle\,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket^{(2)}\mid g\in\Sigma_{\bullet}\,\big\rangle\subseteq\textbf{{Qubit}},

generated by the interpretations of the gates in
\Sigma_{\bullet}
; equivalently, this is the intended semantic category of the fragment. Since the master interpretation
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{(2)}
restricts to
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}
and lands in
\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\bullet}
, and since every equation of
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
holds semantically, it factors uniquely through the quotient to a well-defined strict symmetric monoidal functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}:\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\bullet}
.

When
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
is faithful we say that the presentation
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}
is
complete
for the corresponding fragment (in the sense of
\cref
def:semantic-completeness).

We now instantiate this scheme for each qubit fragment.

Definition 16
.

The five qubit fragments treated in this paper are specified by the following
sub-signatures and rule sets:

Fragment

\Sigma_{\bullet}\subseteq\Sigma^{(2)}

Rule set
\textup{C}_{\bullet}

Clifford

\Sigma_{\mathit{Cliff}}\coloneqq\{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\}

\textup{C}_{\mathit{Cliff}}\coloneqq\textup{Cliff}_{2}
(
\cref
fig:qubitcliffordaxioms)

Real Clifford

\Sigma_{\mathit{RCliff}}\coloneqq\{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/minus.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/Z.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\}

\textup{C}_{\mathit{RCliff}}\coloneqq\textup{RCliff}_{2}
(
\cref
fig:qubitrealcliffordaxioms)

Clifford
+T

\Sigma_{\mathit{CliffT}}\coloneqq\{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/T.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\}

\textup{C}_{\mathit{CliffT}}\coloneqq\textup{CliffT}_{2}
(
\cref
fig:qubitcliffordplustaxioms)

Clifford
+CS

\Sigma_{\mathit{CliffCS}}\coloneqq\{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CS.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\}

\textup{C}_{\mathit{CliffCS}}\coloneqq\textup{CliffCS}_{2}
(
\cref
fig:qubitcliffordpluscsaxioms)

CNOT-dihedral

\Sigma_{\mathit{CNOTdihe}}\coloneqq\{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/alpha.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/X.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/T.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\}

\textup{C}_{\mathit{CNOTdihe}}\coloneqq\textup{CNOTdihe}_{2}
(
\cref
fig:qubitdihedralaxioms)

These data determine the five presented PROPs
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}
(cf.
\cref
def:qubit-fragment-scheme).
For each fragment
\bullet
, restricting the fixed interpretation
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{(2)}

yields the PROP morphism

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}:\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\bullet}

defined above.

3.2
The qutrit Clifford fragment

For qutrits, we only consider the Clifford fragment. We therefore present it directly, without introducing
a separate “qutrit master signature”. We use the computational basis
\{\left|0\right\rangle,\left|1\right\rangle,\left|2\right\rangle\}
of
\mathbb{C}^{3}

(and the induced tensor-product basis of
(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\otimes n}
).

Definition 17
.

The
qutrit Clifford
fragment is generated by the signature

\Sigma_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\coloneqq\{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/wb.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:0\to 0,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:1\to 1,\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}:2\to 2\}.

Let
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}_{\Sigma_{\mathit{Cliff3}}}
be the free PROP on

\Sigma_{\mathit{Cliff3}}
. Let
\textup{C}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\coloneqq\textup{Cliff}_{3}
be the rule
set given in
\cref
fig:qutritcliffordaxioms. We then define

\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}/\textup{C}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}.

By the universal property of the free PROP, there is a unique strict symmetric
monoidal functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathit{Cliff3}}:\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\longrightarrow\textbf{{Qutrit}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}

determined by

\begin{array}[]{l l}\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/wb.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathit{Cliff3}}:1\longmapsto e^{i\pi/6}&\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/H.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathit{Cliff3}}:\left|x\right\rangle\longmapsto\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\!\sum_{k=0}^{2}e^{2\pi i\,xk/3}\left|k\right\rangle\\[6.0pt]
\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/CNOT.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathit{Cliff3}}:\left|x,y\right\rangle\longmapsto\left|x,(x+y)\bmod 3\right\rangle&\left\llbracket\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\right\rrbracket_{\mathit{Cliff3}}:\left|x\right\rangle\longmapsto e^{i\pi x(x-1)/3}\left|x\right\rangle\end{array}

for
x,y\in\{0,1,2\}
. Here

\textbf{{Qutrit}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\coloneqq\langle\,\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\mid g\in\Sigma_{\mathit{Cliff3}}\,\rangle

is the semantic subPROP generated by the qutrit Clifford gates.

\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/wc.pdf}}}}}}}$}}^{\otimes 8}=\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/empty.pdf}}}}}}}$}}

4
Completeness: from PROs to PROPs

For each fragment
\bullet
introduced in
\cref
sec:circuits-relations we have a presented PROP

\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}=\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}/\textup{C}_{\bullet}

and a strict symmetric monoidal interpretation functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}:\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\bullet}
(or
\textbf{{Qutrit}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}
in the qutrit case).
Completeness means faithfulness of

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
in the sense of
\cref
def:semantic-completeness.

Complete equational theories for the same fragments are already available in the literature

[
19
,
17
,
15
,
3
,
4
,
1
]
.
The purpose of this section is to explain how completeness of our presentations
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}

is derived from those results without re-proving fragment-specific normal-form theorems.
The argument is uniform across the six fragments: for each fragment we relate our syntax to a
known complete source syntax by explicit translations and then transfer faithfulness.

Since we work in PROPs, whenever a source theorem is formulated in a PRO setting we implicitly
replace it by its PROP lift obtained by making the symmetry structural, using the lifting principle
of
\cref
rem:fromprotoprop. From this point on, all syntactic categories considered in this section
are PROPs and all translations are PROP morphisms in the sense of
\cref
def:prop-morphism;
in particular they are strict symmetric monoidal and are the identity on objects.

With this convention in place, transferring completeness from the literature to our smaller axiom
sets requires two additional ingredients.

1.

We work with strict unitary equality, hence global phases are part of the semantics.
When comparing a source presentation with our target semantics, we must ensure that both
interpretation functors land in the same semantic subPROP of
FdHilb
, including the same
subgroup of scalars. When a source presentation uses a smaller scalar convention, we apply a
conservative scalar refinement (
\cref
subsec:scalar-refinement).

2.

Once source and target semantics are aligned, we construct explicit encoding and decoding PROP
morphisms between the corresponding free PROPs. A general transfer theorem
(
\cref
th:completeness) then transports faithfulness from the source presentation to the target one.

4.1
Scalar refinement

For any PROP
P
, its scalars are the endomorphisms of the tensor unit:

S(\textbf{{P}})\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}(0,0).

S(\textbf{{P}})
is a commutative monoid under composition.
Moreover, scalars are central: for any morphism
f:n\to m
and any scalar
s:0\to 0
,

(s\otimes\mathrm{id}_{m})\circ f=f\circ(s\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}).

In particular, for scalars
s,t:0\to 0
one has
s\circ t=s\otimes t=t\circ s
.

Before comparing presentations by translation, we require that source and target interpret into the
same semantic subPROP of
FdHilb
.
Because we work with strict unitary equality, this alignment involves the scalar subgroup:
for each semantic subPROP
\textbf{{C}}\subseteq\textbf{{FdHilb}}
, the set
S(\textbf{{C}})
records the
global phases visible in that semantics.

Two of the source completeness results use a smaller scalar subgroup than the one fixed in

\cref
sec:circuits-relations:

•

in the qutrit Clifford case, the presentation of
[
15
]
uses an order-
6
scalar
subgroup, whereas our semantics uses an order-
12
subgroup; and

•

in the Clifford+CS case, the presentation of
[
4
]
uses an order-
4

scalar subgroup, whereas our qubit setting uses an order-
8
subgroup.

We therefore apply the scalar-refinement construction of Appendix
I
.
Concretely, scalar refinement adjoins a new scalar generator of the desired order, together with
equations relating it to the original scalar convention, so that the refined syntax interprets into the
same scalar subgroup as our target semantics.
By
\cref
lem:scalar-refinement, this refinement is conservative: it does not introduce new equalities
between non-scalar circuits and preserves faithfulness of the interpretation functor.

4.2
Encoding, decoding, and transfer of completeness

We now isolate the abstract argument used to transport completeness from a known source presentation
to a target presentation.

Let
\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}
be a source presentation equipped with an interpretation

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}:\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}\to\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}\subseteq\textbf{{FdHilb}},

and let
\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}
be a target presentation equipped with

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket:\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}\to\textbf{{C}}\subseteq\textbf{{FdHilb}}.

Definition 18
.

A pair of PROP morphisms

E:\textbf{{P}}\to\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}},\,D:\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}\to\textbf{{P}}

is an encoding/decoding pair if, for all circuits
C\in\textbf{{P}}
and
C^{\prime}\in\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}
,

\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket E(C)\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}
and
\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}=\left\llbracket D(C^{\prime})\right\rrbracket.

The next two lemmas provide a finite checklist for proving that translations can be used to transport
derivations.
The first lemma shows that it suffices to check
D\circ E=\mathrm{id}
on generators.

Lemma 19
.

Assume
P
is presented by generators
\Sigma
and relations
\mathcal{R}
.
If for every non-structural generator
g\in\Sigma
,

\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}\vdash D(E(g))=g,

then for every circuit
C
built from
\Sigma
,

\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}\vdash D(E(C))=C.

Proof.

By structural induction on
C
, using that
E
and
D
preserve
\circ
and
\otimes
and fix all
structural morphisms.
∎

The second lemma ensures that decoding is compatible with the source congruence, so that any
derivation in the source theory can be transported into the target theory.

Lemma 20
.

Assume that for every axiom
L=R
in
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}
,

\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}\vdash D(L)=D(R).

Then
D
induces a PROP morphism

\overline{D}:\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}\to\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R},

and for all circuits
C_{1},C_{2}\in\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}
,

\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}\implies\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}\vdash D(C_{1})=D(C_{2}).

Proof.

The hypothesis states that
D
equalises all relations in
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}
, hence
D
factors
through the quotient.
∎

Remark 21
.

In applications,
E
and
D
are specified on generators and extended uniquely to PROP morphisms by
the universal property of free PROPs.
The hypotheses of
\cref
lem:decenc,lem:decrelations then reduce to a finite list of equalities:
one equation for each target generator and one equation for each source axiom.

Theorem 22
.

Assume
\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}
is complete for
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}
.
Suppose there exists an encoding/decoding pair
(E,D)
in the sense of
\cref
def:encdec satisfying
the hypotheses of
\cref
lem:decenc,lem:decrelations.
Then
\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}
is complete for
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket
.

Proof.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\in\textbf{{P}}
be circuits of the same type with
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket
.
By
\cref
def:encdec,

\left\llbracket E(C_{1})\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}=\left\llbracket E(C_{2})\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}.

Since the source presentation is complete, this implies

\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}\vdash E(C_{1})=E(C_{2}).

Applying
\overline{D}
from
\cref
lem:decrelations yields

\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}\vdash D(E(C_{1}))=D(E(C_{2})).

Finally,
\cref
lem:decenc rewrites both sides to obtain
\textbf{{P}}/\mathcal{R}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
.
∎

4.3
Fragment-wise completeness

For each fragment, the appendices provide:
a source presentation from the literature,
explicit definitions of
E
and
D
on generators,
and the verification of the checklists in
\cref
lem:decenc,lem:decrelations.
The source presentations are recalled in

\cref
appendix:oldgraphicallanguage1,appendix:oldgraphicallanguage2,appendix:oldgraphicallanguage3,appendix:oldgraphicallanguage4,appendix:oldgraphicallanguage5,appendix:oldgraphicallanguage6.
The encoding/decoding functors are specified on generators in

\cref
tab:ED-clifford-family.
The generator equalities required for
\cref
lem:decenc and the axiom checks required for

\cref
lem:decrelations are proved in Appendices
F
and
G
.

For qutrit Clifford and Clifford+CS we first apply the scalar refinements described in

\cref
subsec:scalar-refinement so that source and target are interpreted in the same semantic subPROP.
We then instantiate
\cref
th:completeness with the encoding/decoding pairs of

\cref
tab:ED-clifford-family.

Theorem 23
.

1.

The graphical language
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{Cliff}}
is complete for

\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\mathit{Cliff}}
.

2.

The graphical language
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{RCliff}}
is complete for

\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\mathit{RCliff}}
.

3.

The graphical language
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{CliffT}}
is complete up to
2
qubits for

\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\mathit{CliffT}}
.

4.

The graphical language
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}
is complete for

\textbf{{Qutrit}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}
.

5.

The graphical language
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{CliffCS}}
is complete up to
3
qubits for

\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\mathit{CliffCS}}
.

For the CNOT-dihedral fragment, the known complete equational theory is already formulated as a
PROP with the same scalar generator as in our master signature
[
1
]
.
Thus no scalar refinement or encoding/decoding step is required.
Completeness of our smaller axiom set is obtained by showing that the axioms omitted from

[
1
]
are derivable from
\textup{CNOTdihe}_{2}
; this derivability is proved in
Appendix
H
.

Theorem 24
.

The graphical language
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\mathit{CNOTdihe}}
is complete for

\textbf{{Qubit}}_{\mathit{CNOTdihe}}
.

5
Minimality

Completeness of a rule set
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
means that
every semantic equality between circuits of the fragment can be derived using
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
together
with the structural equalities of PROPs. In this section we study the complementary question of
non-redundancy: whether the axioms of
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
are all necessary for completeness.

Throughout, fix a fragment
\bullet
with free PROP
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}

and finite rule set
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
.
All non-structural generators are endomorphisms, hence every well-typed circuit has type
n\to n
,
and equational derivations preserve the wire count.

5.1
Independence and minimality

For an axiom
\rho
in
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
we write
\textup{C}_{\bullet}\setminus\{\rho\}
for the remaining axioms,
and we let

\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}^{-\rho}\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}/(\textup{C}_{\bullet}\setminus\{\rho\})

denote the presented PROP obtained by removing
\rho
and closing under congruence
(
\cref
def:presented-prop).

Definition 25
.

Let
\rho
be an axiom
C_{1}=C_{2}
in
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
(with
C_{1},C_{2}:n\to n
).
We say that
\rho
is independent of the other axioms of the same fragment if

\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}^{-\rho}\not\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}.

We say that
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
is minimal if every
\rho\in\textup{C}_{\bullet}
is independent.

Remark 26
.

Assume
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
is sound for an intended semantics
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
.
Then an axiom
\rho\in\textup{C}_{\bullet}
is necessary for completeness (removing it destroys faithfulness of

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet}
) if and only if it is independent in the sense of
\cref
def:minimality.
Indeed, if
\rho
were derivable from
\textup{C}_{\bullet}\setminus\{\rho\}
then removing
\rho
would not change
the induced congruence; conversely, if
\textup{C}_{\bullet}\setminus\{\rho\}
were still complete, then the semantic
validity of
\rho
would force
\rho
to be derivable.

Since derivations preserve arity, it is natural to state bounded minimality results by truncating
to a fixed number of wires.

Definition 27
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Define

\textup{C}_{\bullet,\leq k}\coloneqq\{\rho\in\textup{C}_{\bullet}\mid\rho\text{ has type }n\to n\text{ with }n\leq k\}.

We say that
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
is minimal up to
k
wires if every
\rho\in\textup{C}_{\bullet,\leq k}
is independent
relative to
\textup{C}_{\bullet,\leq k}
(i.e. independent in the sense of
\cref
def:minimality after replacing

\textup{C}_{\bullet}
by
\textup{C}_{\bullet,\leq k}
).

Remark 28
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Any equation derivable in the hom-set
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}(n,n)
uses only axioms of
type
m\to m
with
m\leq n
. This follows because both
\circ
and
\otimes
preserve the wire count and
every generator is an endomorphism. Thus, to prove independence of a relation
\rho:n\to n
, it suffices to work in the truncated system

\textup{C}_{\bullet,\leq n}
.

5.2
Separation by alternative interpretations

Our independence proofs are semantic: to show that an axiom
\rho
is not derivable from the others,
we construct a strict symmetric monoidal interpretation into a small target PROP that validates all
axioms except
\rho
.

Lemma 29
.

Let
\rho:C_{1}=C_{2}
be an axiom in
\textup{C}_{\bullet}
.
Assume there exist a PROP
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{alt}}
and a PROP morphism

F:\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{alt}}
such that

\forall(L=R)\in(\textup{C}_{\bullet}\setminus\{\rho\}),\;F(L)=F(R),
but
F(C_{1})\neq F(C_{2}).

Then
\rho
is independent.

Proof.

Since
F
equalises every axiom in
\textup{C}_{\bullet}\setminus\{\rho\}
, it factors through the quotient

\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}^{-\rho}
, yielding a PROP morphism

\overline{F}:\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}^{-\rho}\to\textbf{{P}}_{\mathrm{alt}}
.
If
\textbf{{QCirc}}_{\bullet}^{-\rho}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}
then applying
\overline{F}
gives
F(C_{1})=F(C_{2})
, a contradiction.
∎

Remark 30
.

All morphisms of
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}
are already quotiented by PROP coherence
(
\cref
def:free-prop). Thus, once
F
is a PROP morphism, it automatically respects all structural
equalities. In practice, to apply
\cref
lem:separation it
suffices to check the non-structural axioms of the fragment.

5.3
Families of separating interpretations

We now define the basic target PROPs and interpretation schemes used throughout the independence
arguments collected in
\cref
fig:minimality-all. In figures, some gates may be displayed using
syntactic abbreviations; these abbreviations stand for fixed morphisms of the free PROP, and every
separating interpretation is applied to the expanded morphisms.

Definition 31
.

Let
(M,\oplus,0)
be a commutative monoid and let
\epsilon\in M
satisfy
\epsilon\oplus\epsilon=0
.
Define
\textbf{{End}}_{\epsilon}(M)
to be the PROP with objects
\mathbb{N}
and hom-sets

\textbf{{End}}_{\epsilon}(M)(n,n)\coloneqq M\;(n\in\mathbb{N}),

\textbf{{End}}_{\epsilon}(M)(n,m)\coloneqq\varnothing\;(n\neq m).

Composition and tensor are given by
\oplus
, identities by
0
, and the symmetry is defined by

\sigma_{1,1}\coloneqq\epsilon\in M.

Remark 32
.

The condition
\epsilon\oplus\epsilon=0
implies
\sigma_{m,n}\circ\sigma_{n,m}=\mathrm{id}_{n+m}
.
All other PROP coherence axioms hold because every structural map is interpreted as an element of
the commutative monoid
M
and both
\circ
and
\otimes
are interpreted as
\oplus
.

Definition 33
.

For
k\geq 2
define

\textbf{{Count}}_{k}\coloneqq\textbf{{End}}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}_{k},+,0)
and
\textbf{{Has}}\coloneqq\textbf{{End}}_{0}(\mathbb{B},\vee,0)
with
\mathbb{B}=\{0,1\}.

When we want to record the parity of the structural swap, we also use

\textbf{{Count}}^{\mathrm{swap}}_{2}\coloneqq\textbf{{End}}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},+,0),

so that the basic symmetry
\sigma_{1,1}
is interpreted as
1\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Definition 34
.

Let
k\geq 2
.
Given a finite list
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{r})
of generators in
\Sigma_{\bullet}
(not necessarily distinct), define
w:\Sigma_{\bullet}\to\mathbb{Z}_{k}
by

w(g)\coloneqq\big|\{\,i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}\mid g_{i}=g\,\}\big|\bmod k\;(g\in\Sigma_{\bullet}).

We then write

\#\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}\}_{[k]}\coloneqq F_{w,k}:\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{Count}}_{k}.

When
k=2
, we use the symbol

inside the braces only as a mnemonic that swaps are counted:

\#\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{r},\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SWAP.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\}_{[2]}\coloneqq F^{\mathrm{swap}}_{w}:\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{Count}}^{\mathrm{swap}}_{2}.

In particular,
\#\{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SWAP.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\}_{[2]}
denotes the functor
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{Count}}^{\mathrm{swap}}_{2}
that sends every non-structural generator to
0
.

Definition 35
.

Let
g\in\Sigma_{\bullet}
.
By the universal property of the free PROP, there exists a unique PROP morphism

?g:\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{Has}}

such that
?g(g)=1
and
?g(h)=0
for every generator
h\in\Sigma_{\bullet}
with
h\neq g
.
Equivalently, for any circuit
C
, one has
?g(C)=1
if and only if
g
occurs at least once in
C
.

Projective interpretations are used to forget global phases while keeping the remaining unitary content.

Definition 36
.

Let
\textbf{{PU}}_{d}
be the PROP of projective unitaries on
d
-dimensional systems (unitaries modulo
global phase), and let
\pi_{d}
denote the quotient map from unitaries to their projective classes.
For each fragment
\bullet
, define the projective interpretation

J^{\mathrm{proj}}_{\bullet}\coloneqq\pi_{d}\circ\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\bullet},

with
d=2
in the qubit case and
d=3
in the qutrit case.

Given a generator
g:n\to n
and a circuit
C:n\to n
, we write

g\mapsto C

for the PROP morphism
\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\to\textbf{{PU}}_{d}
obtained by sending
g
to

J^{\mathrm{proj}}_{\bullet}(C)
and sending every other generator
h\neq g
to
J^{\mathrm{proj}}_{\bullet}(h)
.

The last family of separators records a scaled determinant phase, chosen so as to be strictly monoidal
on a bounded number of wires.

Definition 37
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and write
\arg\det(U)\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
for the phase of
\det(U)
.
Set

\epsilon_{k}\coloneqq 2^{k-2}\pi\bmod 2\pi,

so
\epsilon_{2}=\pi
and
\epsilon_{k}=0
for
k>2
.

Define a PROP morphism

\arg\det_{k}:\textbf{{P}}_{\bullet}\longrightarrow\textbf{{End}}_{\epsilon_{k}}(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z},+,0)

on generators
g:n\to n
by

(\arg\det_{k})(g)\coloneqq 2^{k-n}\cdot\arg\det(\left\llbracket g\right\rrbracket)\bmod 2\pi,

and extend it to all circuits using preservation of
\circ
,
\otimes
, and symmetries.

Remark 38
.

The scaling factor
2^{k-n}
is chosen so that
\arg\det_{k}
is compatible with tensoring on arities

\leq k
, using
\det(U\otimes V)=\det(U)^{2^{m}}\det(V)^{2^{n}}
for
U
on
n
qubits and
V
on
m
qubits.
The choice of
\epsilon_{k}
ensures that the image of the structural swap
\sigma_{1,1}
coincides with its
determinant phase after scaling: it contributes
\pi
when
k=2
and becomes
0
for
k>2
.

5.4
Minimality statements

Theorem 39
.

The following rule sets are minimal for the indicated fragment-circuits:

1.

\textup{Cliff}_{2}
is minimal for
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{Cliff}}
-circuits (all arities).

2.

\textup{RCliff}_{2}
is minimal for
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{RCliff}}
-circuits (all arities).

3.

\textup{Cliff}_{3}
is minimal for
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{Cliff3}}
-circuits up to
2
qutrit wires.
1
1

1

The only missing case is separating the
3
-qutrit axiom
\cref
qt-I; we conjecture full minimality.

4.

\textup{CliffT}_{2}
is minimal for
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{CliffT}}
-circuits up to
1
qubit wire.

5.

\textup{CliffCS}_{2}
is minimal for
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{CliffCS}}
-circuits up to
2
qubit wires.

6.

\textup{CNOTdihe}_{2}
is minimal for
\textbf{{P}}_{\mathit{CNOTdihe}}
-circuits (all arities).

Moreover:

•

In
\textup{CliffT}_{2}
, the
2
-qubit axioms (
CPh
), (
B
) and (
CZ
)
are independent.

•

In
\textup{CliffCS}_{2}
, the
3
-qubit axiom (
I
) is independent.

Proof.

For each axiom
\rho
covered by the statement,
\cref
fig:minimality-all provides a PROP morphism

F_{\rho}
that equalises all other axioms of the same or lower arity but distinguishes the two sides of
\rho
.
∎

{subfigure}

Figure 21:
Separating interpretations used to witness axiom independence. For each relation, the right column gives a strict symmetric monoidal functor into a simple target PROP that equalises all other axioms of the same fragment but distinguishes the two sides of this relation (via
\cref
lem:separation). Entries marked ‘–’ are relations for which we provide no separating interpretation.

Proposition 40
.

The axiom (
SS′
) is necessary in
\textup{Cliff}_{3}
.

Proof.

The interpretation
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}
respects all axioms of
\textup{Cliff}_{3}
acting on at most one qutrit, except
\cref
qt-ssprime. For example, both (
S3
) and (
E
) are still satisfied, as illustrated using derivations from the appendix:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritcliffordaxioms/spow3.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}=\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/SXSXSX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket\overset{\eqref{qt-SXSXSX}}{=}\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/I-phomega2.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\sim\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./cliffordaxioms/I.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./cliffordaxioms/I.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritcliffordaxioms/shs.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}=\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/SXHSX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket\overset{\eqref{qt-HSXH}}{=}\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/HHHSXSXHHH.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket

\displaystyle\sim\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritcliffordaxioms/h3s2h3.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}

However, when evaluating both sides of
\cref
qt-ssprime under
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}
, we obtain two inequivalent elements in
\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{C})/{\sim}
:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritcliffordaxioms/ssprime.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/SXKSXK.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket\;\;\overset{\eqref{qt-SXKSXK}}{=}\;\;\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/SpSp.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritcliffordaxioms/sprimes.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket^{\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/S.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}\mapsto\scalebox{.69}{{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/SX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}}}_{\sim}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/KSXKSX.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket\;\;\overset{\eqref{qt-KSXKSX}}{=}\;\;\left\llbracket\scalebox{0.85}{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/./qutritderivations/SS.pdf}}}}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket.

Since all other axioms acting on at most one qutrit remain sound under this interpretation, we conclude that removing
\cref
qt-ssprime would lead to an incomplete theory.
∎

Appendix H
CNOT-dihedral circuits

This appendix supplies the fragment-specific proof obligations for the
CNOT-dihedral completeness statement in
\cref
sec:completeness. Unlike the
other fragments, we do not need an encoding/decoding transfer here: the source
presentation of
29
already lives in a PROP setting with the
same scalar convention as our master signature.

What
does
need to be checked is that our smaller rule set

\textup{CNOTdihe}_{2}
is conservative with respect to the original one: every axiom of

29
that we omit must be derivable from
\textup{CNOTdihe}_{2}
.
The lemmas below provide exactly these derivations, so that the imported
completeness theorem applies unchanged.

Remark 41
.

\cref

cnot-old-R4 follows by the same rewriting used in
the section for Clifford+T.

Appendix I
Scalar refinement

This appendix justifies the
scalar-refinement
step used in

\cref
subsec:scalar-refinement. The short version is the following:
in the strict semantics adopted in this paper,
global phases are part
of the data of the fragment
. Consequently, two syntactic presentations of the
same

gate fragment may nevertheless denote
different
semantic subPROPs if they generate
different subgroups of global phases. When importing completeness theorems stated with a
different scalar convention, we therefore first align scalar subgroups by conservatively
extending the syntax with an additional scalar generator.

The main technical claim proved here is that, under mild assumptions satisfied by all our
unitary fragments, adjoining a new root-of-unity scalar and relating it to an existing one is
a
conservative extension
: it does not introduce any new equalities between circuits
that do not mention the new scalar. Equivalently, the induced interpretation remains faithful.

Definition 42
.

Let
P
be a PROP. Its
scalars
are the endomorphisms of the tensor unit,

S(\textbf{{P}})\coloneqq\textbf{{P}}(0,0).

Since
0
is the monoidal unit, both
\circ
and
\otimes
restrict to binary operations

S(\textbf{{P}})\times S(\textbf{{P}})\to S(\textbf{{P}})
,
and they coincide by interchange. In particular,
S(\textbf{{P}})
is a commutative monoid
(and a group whenever
P
is a groupoid).

Definition 43
.

Let
C
be a (strict) symmetric monoidal subcategory of
FdHilb
and assume

C
is
endomorphism-only
:

\textbf{{C}}(n,m)=\varnothing
for
n\neq m
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a unitary

U\in\textbf{{C}}(n,n)
is a
global phase on
n
wires
if it is a scalar multiple of
the identity:

U=\lambda\mathrm{id}_{n}\quad\text{for some }\lambda\in\mathrm{U}(1)=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|z|=1\}.

In words:
U
acts trivially on all states and only contributes a phase factor
\lambda
.

Definition 44
.

Let
\textbf{{C}}\subseteq\textbf{{FdHilb}}
be a symmetric monoidal subcategory.
The
visible scalar subgroup
of
C
is

S(\textbf{{C}})\coloneqq\textbf{{C}}(0,0)\subseteq\mathrm{U}(1).

Every element
\lambda\in S(\textbf{{C}})
yields a global phase on
n
wires by tensoring:

\lambda\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}=\lambda\mathrm{id}_{n}\in\textbf{{C}}(n,n)
.

Definition 45
.

Let
\textbf{{C}}\subseteq\textbf{{FdHilb}}
be an endomorphism-only symmetric monoidal category.
A phase
\lambda\in\mathrm{U}(1)
is called a
hidden phase of
C
if there
exists some arity
n\geq 1
such that
\lambda\mathrm{id}_{n}\in\textbf{{C}}(n,n)
while

\lambda\notin S(\textbf{{C}})
.

Equivalently,
\lambda
is hidden if it occurs as a global phase at some positive wire count,
but cannot be produced as a scalar morphism
0\to 0
inside
C
.

Example 46
.

Consider the subgroup
\textbf{{C}}\subseteq\textbf{{Qubit}}
generated (as a symmetric monoidal
subcategory) by a single 1-qubit gate
R_{X}(2\pi)
, but
with no scalar generators
.
Semantically,
R_{X}(2\pi)=-\mathrm{id}_{1}
, hence
-\mathrm{id}_{1}\in\textbf{{C}}(1,1)
. However,

S(\textbf{{C}})=\{1\}
because there are no nontrivial
0\to 0
morphisms available.
Thus
\lambda=-1
is a hidden phase in the sense of
\cref
def:hidden-phase.

If we were to adjoin a new scalar generator
\omega
interpreted as
-1
, then
\omega\otimes\mathrm{id}_{1}

and the existing circuit
R_{X}(2\pi)
would have the same semantics; without additional axioms
linking them, this would typically destroy faithfulness of the interpretation. The “no hidden phases”
assumption used below precisely rules out this situation.

The conservativity argument relies on the observation that scalars commute with all endomorphisms,
so new scalar generators can always be “pulled out” as a single global phase factor.

Lemma 47
.

Let
C
be a strict symmetric monoidal category. For any scalar
a:0\to 0
and any
endomorphism
f:n\to n
one has

(a\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ f=f\circ(a\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}).

(5)

Moreover, for scalars
a,b:0\to 0
, composition and tensor coincide:

a\circ b=a\otimes b.

Proof.

Since the tensor is bifunctorial and the category is strict,

(a\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ f=(a\circ\mathrm{id}_{0})\otimes(\mathrm{id}_{n}\circ f)=(\mathrm{id}_{0}\circ a)\otimes(f\circ\mathrm{id}_{n})=f\circ(a\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n}),

using the interchange law.
For scalars, the same computation with
n=0
yields
a\circ b=a\otimes b
.
∎

We now formalise the scalar-refinement step as an extension of a presentation by adjoining a new
scalar generator of higher order.

Definition 48
.

For
m\geq 1
write

\mu_{m}\coloneqq\{e^{2\pi ik/m}\mid 0\leq k<m\}\subseteq\mathrm{U}(1)

for the cyclic group of
m
-th roots of unity.

Definition 49
.

Let
\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}
be a presented PROP and let

s:0\to 0
be a chosen scalar in the
src
signature.
Fix integers
m\geq 1
and
\ell\geq 1
.
Define
\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}
to be the
presentation obtained by adjoining a new scalar generator

\omega:0\to 0

and adding the two relations

\omega^{m\ell}=\mathrm{id}_{0},\qquad\omega^{r}=s,

(6)

where
\omega^{k}
denotes the
k
-fold composite of
\omega
(equivalently,
k
-fold tensor),
and
r
is an integer chosen so that semantically
\omega^{r}
matches
s
(see below).

Remark 50
.

The relations (
6
) assert that
\omega
has order dividing
m\ell
,
and that the
src
scalar
s
is the
r
-th power of
\omega
. Concretely, this is the
standard “adjoin a root” construction at the level of scalars. The point of

\cref
lem:scalar-refinement below is that, in a PROP setting, adjoining such a root is
conservative provided there are no hidden phases.

The centrality of scalars yields a simple normal form in the refined syntax: all occurrences of

\omega
can be extracted into a single scalar factor.

Lemma 51
.

In the refined PROP
\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}
,
every morphism
C:n\to n
is derivably equal to one of the form

C=\omega^{a}\otimes C^{\prime},

(7)

where
a\in\{0,1,\dots,m\ell-1\}
and
C^{\prime}
is a morphism built using only the

src
generators (i.e.
C^{\prime}
contains no occurrence of
\omega
).

Proof.

Work in the free PROP on the refined signature, modulo PROP coherence and the relations

\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}
and (
6
). Because
\omega
is a scalar,
every occurrence of
\omega
in a diagram is a
0\to 0
component tensored somewhere with an
endomorphism. By
\cref
lem:scalar-centrality, we can slide each such component past all other
boxes and past all compositions/tensors so as to collect all
\omega
-factors together as a single
power
\omega^{a}
tensored with a diagram
C^{\prime}
that contains no
\omega
.
Finally, use
\omega^{m\ell}=\mathrm{id}_{0}
to reduce
a
modulo
m\ell
into the stated range.
∎

We can now state and prove the main lemma used in
\cref
subsec:scalar-refinement.

Lemma 52
.

Let
\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}
be a graphical language equipped with a

faithful
(i.e. complete) strict symmetric monoidal interpretation

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}:\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}\longrightarrow\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}\subseteq\textbf{{FdHilb}}.

Assume the following semantic properties.

1.

(finite cyclic visible scalars)
The scalar group
S(\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}})=\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}(0,0)
is finite cyclic of order
m

and generated by
\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}
for some chosen src scalar
s:0\to 0
.
Equivalently,
S(\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}})\cong\mu_{m}
.

2.

(no hidden phases)

\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}
has no hidden phases: for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
\lambda\in\mathrm{U}(1)
,
if
\lambda\mathrm{id}_{n}\in\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}(n,n)
then
\lambda\in S(\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}})

(cf.
\cref
def:hidden-phase).

3.

(invertibility)
Every morphism in
\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}
is invertible, i.e. each hom-set

\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}(n,n)
is a group under composition.
3
3

3

In all applications in this paper,
\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}
consists of unitaries, hence this holds automatically.

Fix
\ell\geq 1
and choose a root of unity
\zeta\in\mathrm{U}(1)
of order
m\ell
.
Choose an integer
r
such that

\zeta^{r}=\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}.

(8)

Form the scalar-refined presentation

\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}
as in

\cref
def:scalar-refined-presentation.

Let
\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp}\subseteq\textbf{{FdHilb}}
be the subPROP generated by
\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}

together with the scalar
\zeta:0\to 0
(viewed as the unitary map
\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
,
1\mapsto\zeta
).

Then the interpretation functor extends uniquely to a strict symmetric monoidal functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}:\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}\longrightarrow\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp}

sending
\omega
to
\zeta
, and
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}
is faithful.

In particular, the refined language is complete for
\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp}
and

S(\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp})\cong\mu_{m\ell}
.

Proof.

(1) Soundness and existence of the refined interpretation.
By construction,
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}
agrees with
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}
on all src generators.
We interpret the new generator
\omega
by the scalar
\zeta
.
The additional relations (
6
) hold semantically because

\zeta^{m\ell}=1
and
\zeta^{r}=\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}
by (
8
).
Therefore the universal property of presented PROPs yields a unique induced functor

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}
into
\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp}
.

(2) Scalar extraction in the refined syntax.
By
\cref
lem:scalar-extraction, every refined circuit
C:n\to n
is derivably equal to

\omega^{a}\otimes C^{\prime}
with
0\leq a<m\ell
and
C^{\prime}
an src circuit.

(3) Faithfulness.
Let
C_{1},C_{2}:n\to n
be refined circuits such that

\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}
.
Write both in scalar-extraction normal form:

C_{i}=\omega^{a_{i}}\otimes C_{i}^{\prime}\qquad(0\leq a_{i}<m\ell),

with
C_{i}^{\prime}
containing no
\omega
.
Let
U_{i}\coloneqq\left\llbracket C_{i}^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}\in\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}(n,n)
.
The semantic equality becomes

\zeta^{a_{1}}U_{1}=\zeta^{a_{2}}U_{2}.

(9)

By invertibility
3
, we can right-multiply by
U_{2}^{-1}
to obtain

\zeta^{a_{2}-a_{1}}\mathrm{id}_{n}=U_{1}\circ U_{2}^{-1}\in\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}(n,n).

(10)

Thus
\zeta^{a_{2}-a_{1}}
appears as a global phase in
\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}
on
n
wires.
By the no-hidden-phases assumption
2
, this implies

\zeta^{a_{2}-a_{1}}\in S(\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}}).

By
1
, there exists
t\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}
such that

\zeta^{a_{2}-a_{1}}=\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}^{t}.

(11)

Combining (
9
) with (
11
) gives

\left\llbracket C_{1}^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}=\left\llbracket s^{t}\otimes C_{2}^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}.

Since
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}
is faithful by hypothesis,

\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}\vdash C_{1}^{\prime}=s^{t}\otimes C_{2}^{\prime}.

The refined presentation contains all src relations, so the same derivation is valid in

\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}
.
Now use the refinement relation
\omega^{r}=s
to rewrite
s^{t}=\omega^{rt}
, yielding

C_{1}=\omega^{a_{1}}\otimes C_{1}^{\prime}=\omega^{a_{1}}\otimes\omega^{rt}\otimes C_{2}^{\prime}=\omega^{a_{1}+rt}\otimes C_{2}^{\prime}.

It remains to compare the exponents. From (
11
) and (
8
) we have

\zeta^{a_{2}-a_{1}}=\zeta^{rt}
, so
\zeta^{a_{2}-a_{1}-rt}=1
. Since
\zeta
has order
m\ell
, this implies

a_{2}-a_{1}-rt\equiv 0\pmod{m\ell}.

Using
\omega^{m\ell}=\mathrm{id}_{0}
in the refined syntax, we conclude
\omega^{a_{1}+rt}=\omega^{a_{2}}
, hence

C_{1}=\omega^{a_{2}}\otimes C_{2}^{\prime}=C_{2}.

Therefore
\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\sharp}
is faithful.

(4) Scalars of
\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp}
.
By definition,
S(\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp})
is generated by
S(\textbf{{C}}^{\mathrm{src}})=\langle\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}\rangle

together with
\zeta
. Since
\left\llbracket s\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}=\zeta^{r}\in\langle\zeta\rangle
, we obtain

S(\textbf{{C}}^{\sharp})=\langle\zeta\rangle\cong\mu_{m\ell}
.
∎

Corollary 53
.

With the hypotheses of
\cref
lem:scalar-refinement, let
C_{1},C_{2}
be
src
circuits
(i.e. containing no occurrence of
\omega
). If

\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src},\sharp}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2},

then already

\textbf{{P}}^{\mathrm{src}}/\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{src}}\vdash C_{1}=C_{2}.

Proof.

If the refined theory derives
C_{1}=C_{2}
, then in particular
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}

(because the refined interpretation restricts to the src one on src syntax). Faithfulness of

\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket_{\mathrm{src}}
implies the src derivability.
∎

We now record the two scalar refinements applied in the main text.

•

Qutrit Clifford.

The imported presentation of
[
15
]
uses an order-
6
scalar

. Our strict qutrit convention uses an order-
12
scalar subgroup
(
\cref
sec:circuits-relations). We refine the source syntax by adjoining an order-
12
scalar
generator

and adding the relations

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/wb.pdf}}}}}}}$}^{12}=\mathrm{id}_{0},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/wb.pdf}}}}}}}$}^{10}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/minw.pdf}}}}}}}$}.

This is the
\mu_{6}\to\mu_{12}
refinement referenced in
\cref
subsec:scalar-refinement.

•

Clifford+CS.

The imported presentation of
[
4
]
uses an order-
4
scalar

, whereas our strict qubit convention uses an order-
8
scalar subgroup.
We refine the source syntax by adjoining an order-
8
scalar generator

and adding the relations

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/w.pdf}}}}}}}$}^{8}=\mathrm{id}_{0},\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/w.pdf}}}}}}}$}^{2}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[clip,trim]{figures/gates/i.pdf}}}}}}}$}.

This is the
\mu_{4}\to\mu_{8}
refinement referenced in
\cref
subsec:scalar-refinement.

In both cases, the hypotheses of
\cref
lem:scalar-refinement are satisfied for the unitary
semantics considered in this paper (invertibility is automatic, visible scalars are finite cyclic by
construction, and the imported fragments do not exhibit hidden phases relative to their chosen
scalar conventions). Therefore, scalar refinement preserves faithfulness, and the refined imported
presentations may be treated as complete presentations for the strict semantics used throughout.

Encoding/decoding pairs used in the completeness transfer arguments.
